- **The map (item 125).** When Mass Ride is selected, the map overlay shows the flow score, throughput in riders per minute at the measured compressed state (the headline above, not a cruising figure), instead of the LTS colours, with its own legend and a sequential colour scale from low to high flow. Mass Ride Avoid marks are drawn distinctly on top of it. It fits the existing stress-tile pipeline rather than beside it: the score is a separate tile layer or a property on the stress tiles, computed from the segment table and pre-drawn the same way into the tile cache, and built for the District only. It draws roads only (item 126): off-road trails, paths, sidepaths and similar trail-class ways are neither shown nor scored in Mass Ride mode, just as Mass Ride routing already leaves them out through the no-trail graph. The same goes for protected bike lanes (item 127): separately mapped cycle tracks and protected lanes are neither shown nor scored, and their width does not count as usable road width in the flow model, consistent with the earlier decision that mass rides do not use protected lanes ("Even protected bike lanes aren't used", Owner amendments).
- **Warning icons at busy crossings (item 138).** On a planned Mass Ride route, every intersection where the route crosses an LTS 3 road gets an orange warning icon, and every one where it crosses an LTS 4 road or an Avoid mark gets a red one, drawn on the route across the crossed road. A left turn across such a road (item 133) is flagged the same way, orange or red by the road it turns across; a left turn across a one-way road, which item 133 does not charge, gets no icon. As with the crossing penalty, the colour comes from the crossed road's ordinary LTS. The route summary lists the icons in route order, with the count of each colour, so an organiser can plan marshals for them. Built (2026-10-01, with item 172's markers for every ride type): the icon and the list are the ordinary planner's, and on a Mass Ride the API colours each by the crossed road's tier.
  - Crossing density (2026-10-03). Item 231, "Leave as is." (every LTS 3/4 crossing stays flagged on Mass Ride, items 138, 171), is superseded the same day by item 233: "Actually, I like merging the runs, that sounds good." On Mass Ride, a run of consecutive signalised crossings along one street merges into a single entry in the junction list and the route description, e.g. "Constitution Ave: 6 signalised crossings, 2 of LTS 4 roads"; unsignalised and very-high-stress-without-signal crossings stay separate; the markers stay on the map, grouping as now. Item 234 refines it: "I'd say we'd want some level of clumpings, especially in DC, given the diagional streets. I'd say something like a quarter mile or so of clumping." Signalised crossings group by distance as well as street: consecutive signalised crossings within about 0.25 mi of the previous one join one group, even where the route changes street or crosses at a diagonal avenue or circle, and the group entry names its span and streets, e.g. "1.0 to 1.6 mi: 6 signalised crossings (17th St, 15th St, ... ), 2 of LTS 4 roads"; unsignalised crossings still stand alone. Built (post-merge, 2026-10-03; Owner amendments, "Mass Ride crossing density", has how): the junction list and both description lists say one entry for each such group, with its span, its count, the first three streets crossed and how many are LTS 4, in its worst member's severity; every crossing keeps its marker.
- **Corkers needed (item 139).** Corkers are riders who hold cross traffic at intersections while the group passes. The ride gets a headline "about N corkers", estimated from the route itself:
  - Which junctions count (item 142, with 400 below): crossings of, and turns onto, LTS 3, LTS 4 or Avoid roads. Minor side streets, LTS 1 and 2, need no corkers.
  - Turns count too (OWNER-DECISIONS 400: "That would need a corker. It's equivalent to a right turn"): a left or right turn onto an LTS 3, LTS 4 or Avoid road needs corkers as a crossing does, though no lanes are crossed. 142's "across lts 3+ roads" means the busy road's tier sets the need. The count, when it is built, counts every major junction of 396 (crossing, left turn or right turn onto an LTS 3+ road); the route chart already says "corkers needed" at each of them.
  - Per counted crossing, the approaches to block: 2 for a two-way cross street and 1 for a one-way. The proposed rule is one corker per approach lane, so a two-lane approach needs 2; a complex junction (a traffic circle, a diagonal avenue meeting the grid) is counted by its actual approaches the same way. A signalised crossing the group enters on green needs one corker per approach whatever its lanes, since the red already holds cross traffic and the corker covers the change to green. Driveways and alleys are not counted.
  - At once: the group's length at the chosen ride size (item 128) decides how many intersections it spans at the same time. A window the length of the group is slid along the route, and the most corkers any one window needs is the number needed simultaneously.
  - Relief: corkers leapfrog, released when the tail passes and riding up past the group to the next intersection ahead, so while one set holds, another is in transit. A rotation factor, proposed at 2, covers that, and the headline is the simultaneous number × the rotation factor, rounded up.
  - Worked example, on a sample downtown DC grid stretch with a cross street every 400 ft (120 m), at the worked example's 22 ft road and 7 mph cruise. The assumption is that one cross street in three is LTS 3 or above, so a counted crossing comes every 1,200 ft (370 m), and that each needs 2 corkers: a two-way road, signalised as busy roads almost always are (item 135) and entered on green. A window the length of the group at cruise spans at most one more crossing than it holds whole lengths of the spacing: at 500 riders the group is about 1,560 ft (474 m) long and spans at most 2 counted crossings, so 4 corkers at once and about 8 for the ride (the earlier figures, from the superseded spacing, were 2 and 4). At 2,000 riders it is about 6,220 ft (1.9 km) and spans at most 6, so 12 at once and about 24 for the ride (earlier: 4 and 8). Every figure follows the flow model above, so it moves with the owner's answers to its parameters.
- **Route profile (item 147).** A chart along the route's distance, in miles, with three tracks sharing that axis so each point lines up across them:
  - Rider flow: throughput in riders per minute along the route, with the bottleneck marked and the requirement for the chosen ride size (item 128) drawn as a line, so every stretch that falls below it shows at a glance.
  - Elevation, in feet, from the 3DEP elevation already in the pipeline.
  - Corkers: a marker at each busy crossing or turn onto a busy road (400) with the corkers it needs, orange or red to match the warning icons (item 138), and the running count needed at once as the group moves along the route.
  Hovering the chart highlights that place on the map.
- **Data sources.** OSM `lanes`, `width`, `parking:lane` and `parking:*`, `cycleway`, `highway=traffic_signals` nodes, turn geometry from the graph, and the 3DEP elevation already in the pipeline. Nothing new is downloaded; any source that is would go through the Licensing gate like any other.
- **Relation to existing items.** It builds on the current Mass Ride preset (Presets) rather than replacing its routing: the preset's facility-ignoring behaviour stays ("Mass rides don't need to consider these", Owner amendments), and so do the Key and Memorial Bridge roadway decisions and the other bridge-access decisions, including the Frederick Douglass Bridge at tier 5. It is the Mass Ride map view that item 79 deferred to the backlog. Item 29's elevator-first Metro entrances are unaffected, since they serve rides that start at a station and not a field that takes the roadway.
- **Parameters for the owner to confirm.**
  - The ride size slider: its default (500 proposed; the range is 100 to 2,000, item 129), and the throughput and holding time at which a road counts as poor for a given size.
  - The group's cruising pace: 6 to 8 mph, settled by the owner (item 174), and whether one figure (7 mph) is shown or the range.
  - The compressed state: one rider to 29 ft² [0.37 riders/m²] at 4.3 mph, as September measured it (one to 24 ft² at the tightest; one to 16 ft² packed solid, the owner's estimate), with the density falling as the pace rises (August, 6.5 mph: 0.71 riders a metre of road, about 126 a minute), from the owner's measured video (items 173 and 175, indicative, ±25%), and where a road counts as compressed (turns, signal queues, pinches).
  - Utilisation of the width, about 0.7, and whether it differs by road width or lane count.
  - Whether to commission purpose-shot overhead video of a straight section at cruising pace, to measure the cruising density that no clip has.
  - The surface curve (item 157): the PCI steps, the throughput reductions and the cost per 100 ft, the age discount, and the OSM fallback.
  - The group scale of the intersection penalties (item 171): the base riders (500), and the bounds of one half to three times.
  - Default lane width where OSM has none, by highway class, and whether a parking lane counts as usable (occupied at ride time or not).
  - Whether a median splits the capacity into two streams or the ride uses one side only.
  - The climbing pace model and the length factor for grade.
  - The signal defaults: a 90 s cycle and a 50% red share, with the average red wait taken as half the red.
  - How sharp or narrow a turn has to be to cost capacity, and the routing turn penalty.
  - The transition penalty: the step-down and step-up weights (step-down proposed heavier) and whether changes below some threshold are ignored.
  - Whether Mass Ride Avoid marks are a new category on the stress rows or their own kind.
  - The corker rule: that only crossings of, and turns onto, LTS 3, LTS 4 and Avoid roads count (items 142, 400), approaches per cross street (2 two-way, 1 one-way), one corker per approach lane, one per approach at a signalised crossing entered on green, how complex junctions are counted, the rotation factor (2 proposed), and whether spares are added on top.
- **Open questions.** Bridges and pinch points, where one short narrow stretch sets the whole route's number; turning radii at corners and circles; one-way pairs, and whether a ride may use both directions of a pair; routing as a group, so the field is never split with gaps at a merge; whether, since a long field holds each intersection for its length ÷ pace, the route should prefer fewer and wider signalised crossings. How the map shows the score is settled by item 125, above.

Events with dates, pacing, and RSVPs are out of scope.

## Quality bar

- **Unit tests** for the stress classifier, the conflation step, the jurisdiction tagger and crossing rules, the border control node insertion and crossings table, the directional conditional access remap for the parkway reversal, the Lua remap producing the intended Valhalla attributes for each stress tier, the turn model and left-turn classifier, and the cue type mappings per format.
- **Reference routes** live in `fixtures/reference-routes/` with a README recording their measured properties. Fifteen real routes are supplied across four sets, all anonymized before commit, and the README carries the measured table for each. Coverage outside the District is better than a first reading suggests, and the gap that remains is narrower and differently shaped. All three trail routes run well into Maryland, one of them from the District to Annapolis and BWI across 76 miles, so the Maryland stress layer and the manager-layer permitting are exercised. All three rural group rides are in Loudoun County, so the VDOT volume conflation is exercised. Trailmaxxing and Group Ride therefore each have references on both sides of the District line and the reproduction threshold applies to both in full.
  Mass Ride is the exception, and it is not a gap the owner can close. All four mass rides are in the District, because that is where the owner has organized: a mass ride outside DC runs under a different permitting posture, and the pace, field size, and marshalling practice that shape a route there are things the organizers who run them know and this project does not. So the Mass Ride preset is tuned on District geometry and says so. Its reproduction invariant applies to its District references, since those are real routes and the preset must still reproduce them. What is not claimed is that its defaults transfer: an owner planning a mass ride outside the District sees that the preset was tuned on DC rides and that the jurisdiction cards, not the preset, carry what is different. Reference routes from mass ride organizers in Maryland and Virginia are a standing request, and adopting them is an owner decision taken with those organizers rather than inferred from the routes alone. Four DC Bike Party mass rides are the ground truth for the Mass Ride preset. They bound it in ways the preset had left open: 5 to 7.5 miles, 63 to 164 feet of gain with brief pitches near 6 percent, 2.3 to 4.7 turns per mile, entirely within the District, point-to-point from a fixed start with a near-loop as the occasional case, and no route revisiting its own line. Three real trail routes are also supplied, of 15, 32, and 76 miles, and they bound Trailmaxxing quite differently: 701 to 2333 feet of gain, pitches to 12 percent, and a crossing of the District line on every one. Two design decisions rest on them. Grade tolerance is per-modality rather than global, since one cap would either reject these or be useless for mass rides. And the state-crossing penalty is rightly off by default for every modality including Mass Ride, because all three of these would fail to be reproduced with it on and because crossing a line is reported rather than avoided. Turn density, at 2.3 to 2.9 per mile, is the one measure that does not separate the two sets, so turn count alone never distinguishes a trail route from a road one. Five group rides complete the set. They are rides of roughly twenty at riding pace, which is why they sit between the other two presets: 12 to 35 miles, 347 to 985 feet of gain, pitches to 11 percent, and four of the five inside the District in these city examples. The fifth, Purple Line, is not: it is 51 percent in Maryland and crosses the District line four times, out into Prince George's at mile 8.84 and back in at 18.89, out into Montgomery at 20.25 and back in at 28.16, which is DC to Prince George's to Montgomery to DC to Montgomery to DC and therefore five jurisdiction transitions rather than four, because the re-entry at 18.89 is on Eastern Avenue at Silver Spring and Takoma and so comes back out of Montgomery rather than out of the county the route left into, handing over between the two counties inside Maryland where no District line is crossed at all - which the fixtures README records and a test measures from the GPX against the 1791 cornerstones, and which an earlier claim here that all five stayed inside the District, a later one that it crossed twice out and back, and a later one still that named Prince George's at the 18.89 re-entry, did not survive. Three rural Loudoun rides cover the other setting: 24 to 30 mile loops climbing 74 to 91 feet per mile and reaching 19 percent, which is where Group Ride's grade tolerance comes from since a cap drawn from city riding would reject half a club's calendar. Turn density separates no preset, but it does separate setting: the same club's rural rides run 1.0 to 1.1 turns per mile against 2.4 to 3.3 in the city. The cause is the road network rather than the riding, since a rural route has far fewer roads to turn onto, which makes the measure a property of where a route is more than of what it is. Two consequences follow. Nothing in the application may infer a modality from it. And a turn penalty does correspondingly less work in a sparse network than in a grid, so any comparison of turn counts between routes must normalize by local road density or compare only routes in the same setting.
  One of them corrects a classification that looks obvious and is wrong. Three of the five are named Critical Mass, and by rider count their classification would be arguable, but under the roadway-takeover definition they are group rides. The definition does work the name cannot, so nothing in the application infers a modality from a route's title or headcount. For each, routing a derived waypoint set with its intended modality must reproduce the reference geometry within a buffer. The waypoint set has to be defined, because the fixtures arrive as tracks and carry no waypoints apart from December's `Midpoint`, so routing start to finish and expecting 80 percent overlap with a hand-drawn parade route or a 76 mile trail route would measure nothing. It is the route's control points plus N vias sampled at even spacing from the matched shape, with N recorded per fixture in the README, and the threshold is stated as a function of N so the test measures the router rather than the sampling, measured in both directions so neither a shortcut nor a detour passes: at least 80 percent of the generated route's length lies within 82 ft (25 m) of the reference polyline, and at least 80 percent of the reference lies within 82 ft (25 m) of the generated route. The fixtures README defines gain, turns, and revisits operationally, with a 10 ft (3 m) hysteresis on gain, which is also the hysteresis the application uses, a 40 degree bearing change over 16 ft (5 m) for a turn, and 82 ft (25 m) apart while more than a quarter mile (400 m) separated along the route for a revisit; those definitions are normative and every invariant below uses them. Turn count has two definitions in play and they are not interchangeable: the geometric one above, and Valhalla's maneuver count from the stats source, which suppresses maneuvers where the road name continues and splits others. Each invariant names which it uses and computes both sides the same way; the reference-route bounds use the geometric definition, and the Fast-against-Default comparison uses maneuver counts on both sides. Fixture gain comes from Strava's elevation values while the application's comes from 3DEP, so the absolute gain bounds carry a systematic offset and are written with headroom rather than to the measured figures exactly. Per-modality invariants asserted on every reference route: Mass Ride includes zero trail-class distance, stays between 4 and 9 miles, keeps gain under 250 feet, carries at most 0.1 mile above the 6.5 percent grade cap and nothing above 8 percent, a cap set from the measured 6.2 and 6.3 percent pitches rather than below them, averages under 6 turns per mile, which the measured 2.4 to 4.7 leaves real headroom against, and revisits no location, each bound drawn from the reference routes with headroom; Group Ride includes zero distance on ways traversed at dismount speed and a trail-class share at most the reference route's own; Beginner includes zero top-tier stress distance, or where the router degraded to a penalty the stats block carries a gap warning and the flagged distance equals the top-tier distance; Recovery gain is at most Default gain; Mountain Goat gain is at least Default gain; a Group Ride with the hills slider toward seek gains more than the same Group Ride with it neutral while keeping its own surface, width, and turn behavior; Fast turn count is at most Default turn count; Trailmaxxing reproduces the trail routes within the same geometry threshold, reports road exposure greater than zero on each, and is not penalized for crossing the District line; Group Ride reproduces its reference routes in both settings, tolerates grades to 19 percent, is never rejected for revisiting its own line, and on the rural references does not penalize unpaved surface.
- **Authorization tests.** A role removed in Discord revokes the matching application permission once the gateway event is processed. A pending member, a timed-out member, and a member of a guild the bot cannot see all resolve to absent standing. A guild marked degraded honors cached standing within the grace period, grants nothing new during it, and lapses to none after it. No Discord token is persisted anywhere. A guild the bot is in but that is not configured grants no standing and produces no cached rows. A cached row older than the grace period grants nothing even with no component running to mark it degraded. A deleted user still present in a guild is not re-cached. After a remap, a user in the old guild but not the new one has no standing, and no standing is granted until the new guild's role mapping is set.
- **Jurisdiction reporting default test.** Jurisdiction reporting depends only on the per-route setting, and its default is computed from modality and purpose exactly as in the table. Explicitly set values survive a modality change, except that switching to Mass Ride clears an explicitly set recreational purpose, which the test asserts separately.
- **Visibility tests.** No admin list view shows a route, comment, or membership row the viewing admin's guild scope excludes, and an instance-admin-only action is refused to a guild admin through the admin as well as through the API. A guild admin cannot edit a membership row, a role mapping, or a route's owning guild through the admin, and the attempt is refused and audited. No account holds a usable password or superuser status, and the admin login form does not answer. An instance admin retains admin access when every configured guild is degraded and the bot is down, and removing the last instance admin is refused. A private route is unreachable by share link, index, queue, bot announcement, and thumbnail. A reviewers-visibility route's thumbnail and metadata are unreachable without login. Only link and public routes serve OpenGraph data and thumbnails unauthenticated, and at `link_requires_login` true only public does. A reviewers-visibility route whose audience is guild A alone is unreachable by a reviewer whose only membership is guild B, and becomes reachable only after guild B is added to the audience, which writes an audit row. A user holding the reviewer role in one audience guild and none in another gets reviewer permission on the route. An in-review route at link visibility and one at public visibility both appear in the Needs review queue of every audience guild. A First Amendment assembly route omits author and club from its public page, OpenGraph data, thumbnail, every export, and its share card, while the audit log and in-guild views still name the actor; a recreational route attributes on all of them. A draft never appears in the index, queue, or Discord posting, and a draft set to public behaves as link, staying out of the public index until it leaves draft. Only public routes appear in the public index and omit `noindex`. A guest can comment on a public route and can do nothing else, including on that same route: no fork, proposal, approval, or confirmation. With `public_guest_comments` off, or with it off for one route, a guest write is refused.
- **Export consistency test.** One compiled route exported as GPX, FIT, TCX, and each cue sheet yields, in each format, exactly the published mapping of the canonical cue list, with order and count preserved except for documented budget drops. Cross-format identity is not asserted, because the mappings are many-to-one at different rates per format. Exporting the same route version twice yields byte-identical files. A route imported from a file carrying an author element, an athlete identifier, a device serial, and a creator string exports with none of them present in any format, asserted by scanning the output bytes, while the OpenStreetMap copyright survives. A social card carries the same distance and elevation gain as the stats block for that version, renders attribution at every size, omits every suppressed control point, and in the start-only style contains no route geometry.
- **End-to-end tests** for: create, save, share link; submit, compare, merge, and reject a proposal including the advanced-base case; comment anchoring surviving a version change; issue confirm feeding the penalty tag after a rebuild; a closure producing an alert on a crossing route; a request with explicit zero crossing costs paying nothing at a state line.
- **Fixtures.** A checked-in list of Potomac and Anacostia crossings with expected roadway and sidepath bike legality, asserted by the legality tagger test. A checked-in list of DC traffic circles (Dupont, Logan, Thomas, Scott, Washington, Columbus, Chevy Chase, Westmoreland, Grant, Sherman, Ward, Tenley, Sheridan, Barney, Randle, and the Lincoln Memorial circle) with expected cue text. Bike legality and roadway-versus-sidepath status verified for every downtown tunnel and circle underpass, every parkway, and every federal reservation in the coverage area, with corrections recorded in the override table and never presented in the UI as legal statements.
- **Latency.** p95 under 3 seconds for the single-pass preview of a 50-mile route on the production host, and under a per-modality configured bound for the full candidate set, with Valhalla calls for the set issued concurrently.

## Working conventions

- **Every phase ends with a review panel.** Owner instruction. When a phase's deliverables are complete, dispatch a panel of independent reviewers across the disciplines that phase touched, and revise until every one of them accepts. A phase is not finished when its code is written and its tests pass; it is finished when the panel accepts it. The panel reviews the code and the tests against this plan, not the plan alone, so a finding is either a defect to fix or a place where the plan was wrong and must be corrected with the reason recorded. Blocking findings are fixed before the phase closes; lesser findings are batched, reconciled where reviewers disagree, and applied before the next phase starts. Reviewer findings carry no authority of their own: one that contradicts an owner decision is rejected on the record rather than quietly adopted, which has already happened more than once.
- Ask before choosing between materially different approaches. Do not ask about routine implementation choices.
- Keep any Valhalla fork as a patch set with a documented rebase procedure.
- Document every external data source with its license, URL, and refresh procedure, and re-verify partner API terms before implementing each integration.
- Partner API registrations are not owner tasks, because push is shelved. Build the download path and the device file formats, which are the delivery answer rather than a stage before one, and do not stub the push adapters or carry a connected-account model against a future that may not come: an unbuilt adapter costs nothing, while a stub has to be kept compiling, migrated, and reasoned about in every authorization change.
- Never present jurisdiction, permit, or access information in the UI as a legal statement.

Deferred work the owner has named, each with the OWNER-DECISIONS items that decided it (the owner's record, OWNER-DECISIONS-2026-09-26b.md, outside the repository). None of it is built. Where an item changed the plan, its quotes are also in Owner amendments, below.

**Next release** (the one after the combined rebuild):

- **FOLLOWUP-STRESS-SUGGEST** (206, 207, 232). 206: "I'd like to have a way for people to select a segment and suggest a revision of the stress level. That's one of the major ones for a beta test." A rider selects a segment on the map and proposes a different stress level with a reason; the admin reviews it and, if accepted, it becomes an approved stress override at the next rebuild. 207, its design: submitting needs Discord sign-in ("Discord sign-in required (Recommended)"); suggestions stay private to the admin until approved ("Private until you approve (Recommended)"); it is built right after this rebuild as the first beta task ("Right after this rebuild (Recommended)"). 232 extends it to junctions: "Also, for the plan with the peer review on route stress, add a mechanism for intersections as well. In some times the road is fine, but the intersection can be especially dangerous." A rider selects a junction (a node) and suggests a danger level or an extra cost with a reason; the admin approves it as a junction override that the intersection model applies, raising or lowering that junction's cost and marker, even where the roads either side are fine. It lands in Collaboration and peer review, above, beside the override table's audited rows.
- **FOLLOWUP-PEER-REVIEW** (214, 232). 214: "Add peer review to the next release as well, the one after this rebuild." Asked what it should cover, the owner answered "Both": (a) signed-in riders the admin trusts (reviewers) see pending stress suggestions and agree, disagree or comment before the admin decides, the suggestions staying hidden from the general public (207); (b) riders rate and comment on routes others have saved and shared. With 232, reviewers agree, disagree or comment on junction suggestions the same way. It builds on Collaboration and peer review, above.
- **FOLLOWUP-A11Y-ROUTE** (215). "Next release (Recommended)". A route edge (casing) colour that keeps every level at 3:1 in both palettes (WCAG 1.4.11; standard LTS 4 is 1.09:1 and the traffic-free violet 1.63:1 today), and the faint busy-road lines at zoom 12-13 and the swatch borders with the switch off, both under 3:1. Its third part, a per-stretch text list of the route's stress levels (1.4.1), is not in this item any more: item 220 pulled it forward, and the route description built it (Owner amendments, 2026-10-02, "Screen-reader users and the route description").

  Implemented as (FOLLOWUP-A11Y-ROUTE, wip/a11y-route; the colour parts, front end only): the item's own words, "A route edge (casing) colour that keeps every level at 3:1 in both palettes (WCAG 1.4.11; standard LTS 4 is 1.09:1 and the traffic-free violet 1.63:1 today), and the faint busy-road lines at zoom 12-13 and the swatch borders with the switch off, both under 3:1." (1) No single casing colour can be 3:1 from every class (the amber, the greens and the unrated grey need a casing darker than luminance 0.045, the violet, LTS 4 and Avoid one lighter than 0.5, and contrast depends on luminance alone), so each route section keeps the blue (a slate with the switch on) as the route's outer casing and gains a one-pixel halo of its own under it, `route-halo`: the tier's own casing (dark under LTS 1 to 3 and the unrated grey, white under LTS 4 and Avoid, black and white with the switch on) and white under the traffic-free violet. As built, every class was at least 4.50:1 from its halo (default palette) and 4.61:1 (colour-blind-friendly, switch on), where it was 1.09:1 to 3.95:1 against the blue. Since FOLLOWUP-STRESS-SALIENCE (item 292's softer LTS 3 casing, #45290a, and the switch no longer blackening a busy tier's casing) LTS 3 is 3.60:1 from its halo (default) and 3.83:1 (colour-blind-friendly, switch on), still over this item's 3:1; the other classes are 4.50:1 or more (docs/DEVELOPMENT.md, "Route halo, faint-road edge and swatch borders", has the table). (2) A faint busy road keeps the owner's 40% opacity and 60% width and gains a thin dark edge as a second cue: a one-pixel ring (`line-gap-width`) at 65% of a near black, at least 3.61:1 from every base-map surface (the line alone is 1.1:1 to 2.6:1). (3) A new token `--swatch-border` (#7a808c light, #8b93a1 dark) for the legend swatches and the stress bar, 3.97:1 and 5.4:1 on the panel and at least 3.60:1 on the soft panel; `--border` is untouched. Contrast that could not be reached: none of the colour contrasts; the faint line's own colour stays under 3:1 by design (the edge is the cue), and the route's blue outer ring is not 3:1 from every class (the halo is). Not done here: the item's third part, which the route description built.
